Annex C


Wargaming
C.1 Wargames are representations of armed conflict in a safe-to-fail
environment, in which people make decisions and respond to the
consequences of those decisions. In the context of operations planning,
wargaming is an instrument designed to develop and improve courses of
actions (COAs).

 UK C.1. The Ministry of Defence’s Wargaming Handbook and
 Influence Wargaming Handbook provide additional context and
 guidance for wargaming.

C.2 General preconditions. Some preconditions have to be met, without
which wargaming will fail or only achieve biased results.

      a. Prepared staff. Well prepared staff must be available. Special
      wargaming personnel should be appointed, for example, a wargaming
      director and a wargaming coordinator. They are responsible for the
      preparation and the conduct in terms of contents and organisation.

      b. Constraints and restraints. Both have to be identified for the own
      conduct of operations as well as for the adversarial forces’ assumed
      COAs.

      c. Operational analysis. Operational analysis is not a mandatory                       C
      precondition for conducting wargaming, but it is described here for
      completeness. Operational analysis includes not only the development
      and application of mathematical models, statistical analyses and
      simulations but also the application of expertise and experience to
      determine quantitative factors for our own and adversarial COAs. The
      results indicate trends and tendencies, and as such are only one factor
      to be considered. The quality of these trends and tendencies depends
      on the quality of the initial factors. These models simplify reality, which
      is especially true for asymmetrical scenarios. The scope of operational
      analysis has to be agreed upon between the coordinator, the head of the
      operations planning group (JOPG), and the operational analysis experts.
      If JOPG and time available, wargaming can benefit from operational
      analysis support from the very beginning, whether it is conducted in a
      computer-based or manual manner.
                                            C-1
                        Edition B Version 1 + UK national elements
Annex C to
AJP-5

             the JOPG, decides which method is applied. There are three basic wargaming
             options: wargaming by phases; wargaming to set decisive conditions; and
             wargaming in segments of the operating environment. These are illustrated in
             Figures C.1, C.2 and C.3 respectively.

             C.4 Preparing and conducting wargaming. This involves determining: the
             desired outcome; the method and scope of the game; and the players and
C            supporting functional participants of the game. The operational situation
             should be prepared, tools for manual or computer-assisted simulation and
             analysis available to use, and rules need to be established. The conduct of
             wargaming is determined largely by the desired outcomes, selected method
             and the scope. Wargaming includes the following.

                   a. Setting conditions. An introduction to set the conditions (the
                   aspects of the operating environment) affecting the operation.

                   b. Wargaming moves. A series of ‘game cycles’ will take place. Each
                   game cycle considers the effects of the action/reaction/counter-action.
                   Each cycle starts with the friendly forces action, based on a situation
                   in which the adversary has the initiative. A cognition phase follows, to
                   provide an assessment of the event. A simplified depiction is given in
                   Figure C.4 and complementary Figure C.5.
                                             C-4
                         Edition B Version 1 + UK national elements
                                                                                               Annex C to
                                                                                                   AJP-5



c. Required products. To conduct wargaming, a complete COA
should be developed. Therefore, the following products, including force
and capabilities, should be prepared: phase chart (including description
of phase, decisive conditions to be achieved, effects and actions,
decision points, operational critical requirement, priority of effort, start
and end), draft risk monitoring plan, synchronisation matrix, initial
operations design, commander’s selection criteria, and the operational
timeline.

                   C.5 Assessment and recording of overall results. An assessment of the
                   probable results of any action – reaction – counter-action typically follows
                   each game cycle within a cognition phase and is used to set conditions for
                   succeeding game cycles. The cognition phase includes, for example, the
                   provision of a final contribution to the wargaming cycle by components’ liaison
C                  officers and functional subject matter experts; provision of an overview by
                   operations assessment experts on effects or decisive conditions achieved
                   or not; determination of the resulting conclusions by the wargaming referee;
                   revision of the conclusions (i.e. opportunities and risks identified, any treatment
                   required leading to adjustments of the COA, branch plans, sequels, decision
                   points or commander’s critical information requirements (CCIRs) by each
                   team-scribe); conclusion of the wargaming cycle by wargaming director. An
                   illustration is given in Figure C.6. Observations and conclusions drawn are
                   recorded in line with the purpose. Typically, these include:

                            •   refinements to the COA and correction of deficiencies;

                            •   additional force/capability requirements/resources;

                            •   casualty estimation;

     •   synchronisation requirements;

     •   Identification of new risks, trigger and/or indicators and possible
         needs for implementing controls;

     •   decision points, branches and sequels required;

     •   decisive conditions and supporting CCIRs;

     •   updates/revisions of the centre of gravity (CoG) analyses of key
         actors, and related inputs to required conditions of CoGs, decisive
         conditions, CCIRs, rules of engagement, etc.;

     •   other lessons identified; and

     •   refinement of actions/measures of performance and effects/measures
         of effectiveness wording by assessment staff.

Annex D


Future planning
considerations
Section 1 – Introduction
D.1 Joint warfighting requires commanders to work through multiple
subordinate and supporting headquarters to orchestrate military activities
across the operational domains and to avoid organising and planning the
domains in isolation. In addition, commanders are required to synchronise
military and non-military activities within their sphere of influence. This Annex
focuses on the new domains of space and cyberspace in multi-domain
operations (MDO) and how MDO planning affects the joint functions and other
important factors.60



Section 2 – Domains
General

D.2 As described in the main publication, there are five domains: maritime,
land, air, space and cyberspace. Whilst commanders are familiar with
maritime, land and air, the development of the space and cyberspace domains
requires additional thought and planning. The following paragraphs describe
the planning characteristics of these new domains.

 UK D.1. The UK recognises the five operational domains as:                                          D
 maritime, land, air, space and cyber.

Cyberspace

D.3 Cyberspace operations planners incorporate information on the
adversaries’ disposition in cyberspace. Disposition includes capabilities,
targets and the physical environment including cyber-personal networks


60 While the MCM-0004-2023, Alliance Concept for Multi-Domain Operations is
endorsed by the MC, this annex regards the operationalisation of MDO still as contingent.

                                                D-1
                            Edition B Version 1 + UK national elements
Annex D to
AJP-5



             as well as maritime, land, air and space capabilities. Commanders rely on
             planners to consider the following.

                   a. Adversary operations in cyberspace that may affect commanders’
                   ability to command and control their operations.

                   b. Profiles of system users, administrators, and their relationships.
                   Organic and non-organic assets, locations and detailed capabilities.

                   c. Lead times for interagency coordination, de-confliction and
                   synchronisation.

                   d. Identification of multinational automated systems weaknesses to
                   cyberspace attacks, which include data poisoning and electromagnetic
                   attacks.

                   e. Requirements for coordination, deconfliction and synchronisation
                   with cyber effects that are provided through sovereign cyber effects
                   provided voluntarily by Allies (SCEPVA).

             D.4 Cyberspace is not geographically bound and offers commanders more
             creative options against NATO's adversaries. Therefore, further development
             of offensive cyberspace operations (provided through SCEPVA) enable NATO
             to be more effective during shaping-contesting-fighting. However, as Nations
             currently retain operational control of offensive cyberspace capabilities, NATO's
             role is very limited to requesting SCEPVA, synchronisation and de-confliction.
             This creates a challenge for NATO commanders when trying to integrate cyber
             effects and activities into multi-domain planning.

              UK D.2. The centrality of the cyber domain forms a key planning
D             factor. This includes knowledge and situational awareness of
              national networks, and those networks upon which there is dependency,
              especially for command, control and the maintenance of communication
              and information systems. Establishing resilience, robust security and
              effective cyber defence measures are considered critical, as is system
              redundancy and fallback capabilities. These in turn must be underpinned by
              a comprehensive risk management process.




                                            D-2
                         Edition B Version 1 + UK national element
                                                                                   Annex D to
                                                                                       AJP-5



Space

D.5 Space planners provide NATO commanders with estimates on the
ways and means to integrate space capabilities into operations, missions and
activities, as well as how to plan for effects and actions affecting the space
domain. While minimising risk and generating opportunities, planners and staff
require an understanding of threats across all adversary space capabilities.
Planners estimate the support required from space capabilities to NATO
operations. Commanders rely on planners to consider the following for the
integration of space effects and activities into multi-domain planning.

      a. Adversary factors that may pose a risk or hazard to friendly space
      capabilities. These risks can exist in orbital, link, user and terrestrial
      segments.

      b. Identification and location of adversary space capabilities to target
      with reversible or non-reversible effects in support of operations.

      c. Environmental factors when developing primary, alternative and
      contingent communication plans. Space planners should synchronise
      the military need with commercial space capabilities by determining
      the level and type of integration and augmentation required to support
      operations.

D.6 Finally, these considerations include the operational requirements of
maintaining a robust recognised space picture, knowledge of space systems
limitations, liaison requirements with national and commercial partners and
legal constraints. The comprehension of effects and actions pertaining to the
space domain is essential in order to inform commanders and their staff in
terms of MDO planning and execution.
                                                                                            D




                                             D-3
                         Edition B Version 1 + UK national elements
Annex D to
AJP-5



             Section 3 – Joint Functions
             General

             D.7 In accordance with AJP-01, Allied Joint Doctrine, there are eight joint
             functions. The relationship between them is described in Figure D.1 below. The
             section describes how multi-domain planning considerations should be taken
             into account within the joint functions.


               Command and control         Intelligence               Informed and directed by




                    Manoeuvre                  Fires


                                 Influence
                                attitude and                          Actions
                                 behaviour


                    Civil-military
                                           Information
                    cooperation




                    Sustainment          Force protection             Supported by



D                               Figure D.1 – The joint function framework

             Manoeuvre

             D.8 The scheme of manoeuvre is a function of a thorough understanding of
             the adversary’s course of action and their available capabilities relative to the
             commander’s mission. After comprehending the challenges and opportunities
             for specific mission objectives, commanders develop their schemes of
             manoeuvre, fires, and logistics. Commanders govern the development of
             supporting plans or annexes and articulate how they visualise accomplishing
             the mission. For a multi-domain scheme of manoeuvre, commanders evaluate


                                             D-4
                          Edition B Version 1 + UK national element
                                                                                    Annex D to
                                                                                        AJP-5



all forms of manoeuvre, e.g. envelopment, turning movement, infiltration,
penetration, and frontal attack while considering the implications on the other
instruments of power. Manoeuvre requires dominance in the electromagnetic
spectrum (EMS) to achieve superiority.

Fires

D.9 Fires planners develop the scheme of fires, concurrently with scheme of
manoeuvre, to generate outcomes for the commander’s plans. These plans
should consider capabilities across the operational domains. Fire planners
should consider the following.

        a. Maximise cross-domain solutions with adjacent friendly forces.

        b. Converge lethal and non-lethal effects from all the operational
        domains.

        c. Evaluate all available fires from within the joint task force and the
        Alliance; recognising the planning and lead times to incorporate fires
        from their origins to targets.

        d. For time sensitive targets implement only the fires that are available
        during periods of hostile acts, influence, or surprise.

Information

D.10 Planners provide NATO commanders with estimates on the ways and
means they can leverage information into operations. With this understanding,
commanders utilise information to disrupt adversary systems and processes.
The commander’s approach to MDO uses all available informational
capabilities to accomplish tactical missions. Commanders rely on information                 D
planners to consider the following areas for MDO planning.

        a. The degree, areas, and focus of artificial intelligence and machine
        learning for data informed decision-making.

        b. Methods, movements, and transmissions of adversary and neutral
        actor information. This includes understanding how enemies and neutral
        actors transfer information and manipulate relevant actor perceptions,
        beliefs, attitudes, and behaviours.



                                              D-5
                          Edition B Version 1 + UK national elements
Annex D to
AJP-5



             Civil-military cooperation

             D.11 The operations objectives may require practical cooperation and
             harmonisation of efforts with non-military actors to ensure military activities
             are enabled/enhanced. A collaborative approach would optimise the delivery
             of converging effects in support of military objectives. The military has
             collaborated with non-military actors in the maritime, land and air domains in
             the past to develop a situational understanding, to protect civilians and enable
             military capabilities, especially in the sustainment functions, maritime security
             and generation of the recognised air picture. In all domains – including space
             and cyberspace – the collaborative use of non-military/commercial capabilities
             can reduce risk and improve the probability of mission success. However,
             the synchronisation of military and non-military activities is a challenge for the
             commander as there is no command relationship between the military and
             non-military actors.

              UK D.3. The UK uses the term ‘outreach’ instead of civil-military
              cooperation, on the assumption that Defence activity will be part of
              a broader cross-government approach, which could include stabilisation,
              support to governance, capacity building and regional/local Defence
              engagement.

             Sustainment

             D.12 Sustainment planners develop the sustainment plans concurrently
             with the scheme of manoeuvre and scheme of fires. Sustainment planners
             account for the available planning and execution time with considerations for
             time sensitive targets, levels of risk, and maximised opportunities. This allows
             sustainment planners to account for available sustainment capabilities and
             capacities from military and non-military sources.
D
             Command and control

             D.13 Commanders develop their operations plans, organise their forces,
             assign responsibilities and unity of actions in pursuit of specific objectives.
             Planners analyse the mission, assess the environment to include the
             electromagnetic environment, comprehend the adversary’s likely actions,
             identify high payoff targets and describe the effects in relation to the mission
             objectives. Commanders direct the command and control to integrate and
             orchestrate diverse but mutually supporting forces across the operational
             domains with appropriate coordination to establish command relationships. In


                                            D-6
                         Edition B Version 1 + UK national element
                                                                                   Annex D to
                                                                                       AJP-5



short, multi-domain operations are conducted at all levels but the nature of the
military action depends on the level of command and control.

D.14 Synchronisation across the domains is a defining feature of multi-domain
execution which commanders will apply differently for each operation because
of the adversary’s situation, delegated authorities, permissions and approved
direct authorisations. There may be times when one subordinate command
has priority for electromagnetic operations, space, intelligence, surveillance
and reconnaissance and counter air whilst other subordinates accept risk.
Commanders and staff across the levels will have varying roles in MDO
planning, orchestration and execution; therefore, the alignment of multi-domain
activities will be complex. However, effective use of MDO can produce shock
and temporary advantages against enemy commanders and their forces.
Commanders can then employ parallel or subsequent multi-domain operations
to pursue and exploit those results.

 Command and control for multi-domain operations
 UK D.4. Modern NATO warfighting requires a command and control model
 that enables rapid, coherent and synchronised multi-domain activity. Mission
 command remains foundational; however, operational-level command and
 control must also support distributed decision-making, delegated authorities
 and integrated cross-domain effects. The joint task force commander
 should establish clear supported and supporting relationships, designate
 component responsibilities for decisive conditions, and employ multi-domain
 coordination mechanisms. Command and control arrangements must be
 resilient, distributed and digitally enabled. Command and control for
 multi-domain operations continues to evolve, with the NATO Command and
 Control Centre of Excellence acting as a focal point for this work within the
 Alliance.
                                                                                            D
Intelligence

D.15 Intelligence and collection planners provide NATO commanders with
estimates on the ways and means they can address intelligence requirements.
This allows NATO commanders to visualise, describe, and direct operations.
During MDO planning, commanders rely on intelligence planners to consider
many factors, including but not limited to the following.

       a. The enemy command and control architecture’s operational
       weaknesses. Commanders examine those gaps and seams that will


                                            D-7
                        Edition B Version 1 + UK national elements
Annex D to
AJP-5



                     complicate enemy decision-making, disrupt leadership structures,
                     and interfere with horizontal and vertical integration. Planners examine
                     the enemy’s speed of decision-making that drives tempo, outpaces
                     opponent actions and dictates the course of combat.

                     b. Disruption of links and nodes. Commanders plan to disrupt the
                     links and nodes that facilitate enemy command, support and supporting
                     relationships. This disruption may allow commanders to generate
                     temporal gaps and exploit seams. Commanders can take advantage of
                     these with delays, disruptions, deceptions, and surprises.

                     c. Enemy dependence on space capabilities and radar
                     detection. These dependencies include capabilities that enhance
                     intelligence, surveillance and reconnaissance. Commanders recognise
                     the adversary uses space-based systems and seek to deny their
                     opponents the use of space. Planners account for the adversaries
                     offensive space operations with defensive space operation efforts.

             Force protection

             D.16 Force protection planners provide NATO commanders with estimates
             on methods to preserve the Allied force’s fighting potential through active
             and passive security and defensive measures, emergency management,
             and response. The protected assets can vary from the physical objects and
             personnel to the protection of information and other intangible, but valuable
             items.61 Commanders direct the degree of asset protection in relation to the
             enemy capability to target them. Allied functions, operational processes,
             physical capabilities, and information systems all require protection to survive
             and accomplish the mission. Commanders rely on planners to consider the
             following areas for multi-domain planning.
D
                     a. The degree to which integration of enemy weapons, sensors,
                     and networks can affect the commander’s plans. Planners catalogue
                     actions that disrupt, slow, or limit one or more of the enemy’s weapons,


             61 Environmental protection planning is a process to ensure appropriate environmental
             protection. By early consideration of the potential negative environmental impacts,
             commanders will become aware of the environmental effects of mission accomplishment
             while alternatives still exist. Environmental damage may be an inevitable consequence of
             operations. The accidentally pollution or contamination of air, soil or water can lead to a risk
             to human health and safety which can lead to a negative impact to own troops or the local
             population. This could negatively affect the operation, directly or indirectly.

                                               D-8
                            Edition B Version 1 + UK national element
                                                                                   Annex D to
                                                                                       AJP-5



       sensors, or network capabilities. These actions provide improved
       protection and survivability profiles.

       b. Planners identify limitations in enemy intelligence, surveillance and
       reconnaissance systems. Commanders can direct actions to identify
       and exploit those limitations combined with ‘hiding in plain sight.’ The
       impact can reduce risk to mission and forces.



Section 4 – Other important factors
General

D.17 Highest payoff targets can be found across all domains in the operating
environment. Of particular importance in the context of MDO are threat
capabilities in space, cyberspace, the EMS and the information environment.
Disrupting, degrading, or destroying these targets maximises an operation’s
likelihood of success. The following paragraphs describe the planning context
for the EMS and strategic communications as well as for two other important
factors: targeting and military deception.

Electromagnetic spectrum

D.18 Freedom of manoeuvre and action within the electromagnetic
environment is essential to Allied operations. Allied electromagnetic operations
(EMO) planners provide commanders with estimates on the ways and
means they can employ the EMS in EMO. Commanders provide subordinate
and component commanders guidance to determine EMS activities and
capabilities that guide their planning. The commander’s EMS planners also
submit EMS-related information requests and ensure plans adhere to the rules
of engagement. Commanders rely on planners to consider the following for                    D
multi-domain planning.

      •   the integration of the EMS to exploit, attack, protect, and manage
          the electromagnetic environment; and

      •   electromagnetic warfare, EMS management, intelligence, space, and
          cyberspace mission areas to prioritise, integrate, synchronise, and
          deconflict EMS.




                                            D-9
                        Edition B Version 1 + UK national elements
Annex D to
AJP-5



             Strategic communications

             D.19 Strategic communications planners provide NATO commanders
             with estimates on the ways and means they can leverage information into
             operations. With this understanding, commanders utilise information to disrupt
             adversary systems and processes. The commander’s approach to MDO uses
             all available informational capabilities to accomplish missions. Commanders
             rely on planners to consider the following areas for MDO planning.

                    a. The degree, areas, and focus of artificial intelligence and machine
                    learning for data informed decision-making.

                    b. Methods, movements, and transmissions of information. This
                    includes understanding how enemies and neutral actors transfer
                    information and manipulate relevant actor perceptions, beliefs,
                    attitudes, and behaviours.

              Defence strategic communication

              UK D.5. The UK refers to Defence strategic communication rather than
              strategic communications. The UK’s definition for Defence strategic
              communication is: ‘advancing national interests by using Defence as a
              means of communication to influence the attitudes, beliefs and behaviours
              of audiences.’ For more information, see Allied Joint Publication-10, Allied
              Joint Doctrine for Strategic Communications, Edition A, Version 1, with UK
              national elements.

             Targeting

             D.20 Commanders select targets that include known enemy strengths,
D            critical capabilities and requirements, and critical vulnerabilities. Commanders
             consider global capabilities to achieve objectives and accomplish the mission.
             Commanders rely on planners to provide the following considerations for
             targeting:

                    •   how best to converge fires across domains, the information
                        environment, and the EMS;

                    •   assessment of the previous multi-domain operations;




                                            D-10
                         Edition B Version 1 + UK national element
                                                                                     Annex D to
                                                                                         AJP-5



       •   prioritising, integrating, and synchronising fires for the targets that
           provide the highest payoff; and

       •   integrating digital backbone derived AI-targeting support solutions.

Military deception

D.21 Planners provide estimates on the ways and means they can decipher
enemy beliefs and preferences, identify how to exploit those preferences,
establish desired perceptions, and focus deception events. Planners determine
Allied force weaknesses from enemy denial and deception. For this reason,
planners focus on the enemy’s efforts to deny and deceive intelligence
collection efforts. Commanders rely on planners to consider, identify, and
develop the following for planning:

       •   deception target(s) and their associated opportunities;

       •   deception stories that are more likely to deceive the enemy; and

       •   deception types, techniques, and tactics.

                         Edition B Version 1 + UK national elements
Annex D to
AJP-5

UK Annex E


Schematics, tables and
matrices
UK E.1. There are several tools and techniques that can be used to present
aspects of a course of action or plan to ensure the clear communication of a
commander’s intent and scheme of manoeuvre. These tools and techniques
are not prescriptive, but the tools should be selected and adapted according
to the context. They can be used both during and after the planning process.

Tools and techniques

UK E.2.   This annex contains seven sections, which explore the following
areas.

          a. Section 1 – Decisive condition/effect tables. Analysis of
          decisive conditions leads to the effects necessary to create them.
          Decisive condition/effect tables, an example of which is at Section 1,
          provide a useful means of depicting these relationships and
          visualising the contribution required, by both the joint force and other
          non-military organisations.

          b. Section 2 – Effects schematics. Effects are an important part
          of the planning process and developing courses of action (COAs);
          they also form the foundation of a joint task force’s synchronisation
          of forces and functions (scheme of manoeuvre) included within
          operation plans (OPLANs) and operation orders (OPORDs). One or
          more effects schematics are a useful technique to describe, by time
          and/or space, the intended effects.
                                                                                                 UK
          c. Section 3 – Integration tables. A vital yet complex part of the
          planning process is identifying activities that will support the creation               E
          of each effect; the use of integration tables can be used to support
          this. It also stimulates alternative activities and is key to the early
          identification of risk.




                                             E-1
                         Edition B Version 1 + UK national elements
 UK Annex E to
 AJP-5



                                         d. Section 4 – Operations planning concepts symbols. A
                                         common set of planning concept symbols, for use in schematics.

                                         e. Section 5 – Schematics. Various options for drawing up
                                         schematics, describing the COA (or plan) through a series of decisive
                                         conditions that will achieve the objectives (and hence the end state).

                                         f. Section 6 – Effects schematics. The clarification of intended
                                         activities is aided by using effects schematics to describe aspects of
                                         the COA (or OPLAN/OPORD).

                                         g. Section 7 – Synchronisation matrix. This section shows an
                                         example of a synchronisation matrix.

                 Section 2 – Effects schematics
                 UK E.3. Effects schematics provide a useful means to visualise and
                 communicate the scheme of manoeuvre, or at least one aspect, by time
                 or space. They can be generated throughout the planning process for
                 developing, evaluating and selecting COAs. They can also be included within
                 OPLANs and OPORDs to aid understanding.

                 UK E.4. The effects included on schematics need not be confined to those
                 from the physical dimension. A schematic is enriched by including effects to be
                 created in the virtual and cognitive dimensions.

Section 5 – Schematics
                                                                                                    E
UK E.6. A schematic is used to aid understanding in planning and executing
an operation. It is of use to a commander in developing and expressing their
theory of change. It is also of use to the staff in transforming ideas into viable
COAs. Finally, it is of use in monitoring and reviewing progress. Hence, its
utility extends across operational design and operations management.



                                                  E-7
                              Edition B Version 1 + UK national elements
 UK Annex E to
 AJP-5

                 emerge dependent on the nature of the crisis and the personal preferences
                 of key commanders and staff. This section provides several examples of
                 operations schematics. They serve to offer alternative options and to stimulate
                 the development of schematics best suited to the context.

Section 6 – Effects schematics                                                                                 UK
UK E.8. Commanders use effects schematics as a framework with which to                                          E
plan, coordinate and synchronise activities to create effects. They should use
the full range of available military and non-military capabilities, including joint
and multinational, and orchestrate fires, information, outreach and manoeuvre
to optimise their coherent impact. They should consider those multi-agency
activities which, while not under their control, might be coordinated with
their own actions. Commanders may establish supported/supporting


                                                    E-13
                                 Edition B Version 1 + UK national elements
 UK Annex E to
 AJP-5



                 interrelationships between their component commanders for each effect,
                 while delegating the maximum freedom of action as to how these effects are
                 created.

                 UK E.9. Effects schematics are a useful means to describe and communicate
                 plans. It is unlikely that a single schematic could depict the entirety of an
                 operation. More likely is that respective operation orders (and supporting plans)
                 will use effects schematics to depict the key actions involved in a phase.

An overview of the
operations planning
process
UK F.1. UK Annex F contains two figures to aid understanding of the
operations planning process (OPP) in relation to the Comprehensive
Operations Planning Directive (COPD). The COPD is Supreme Allied
Commander Europe’s directive on how planning should be conducted by
the NATO Command Structure but with some differences to Allied Joint
Publication (AJP)-5, Allied Joint Doctrine for the Planning of Operations.

         •   UK Figure F.1 shows the stages and outcomes of the OPP
             described in Chapter 3, in relation to the COPD. While operations
             assessment is shown as part of the OPP in the figure, it is
             described separately in AJP-3 and not in AJP-5.

         •   UK Figure F.2 provides an overview of the OPP adding the
             requirement for challenge and red teaming throughout the
             process.

UK Annex G


Operations planning
process
UK G.1. The operations planning process (OPP) is a sequence of planning
activities to examine a mission, develop, analyse and compare alternative courses
of action (COAs) and select the best COA. The process is founded on NATO
Planning Doctrine. Individual headquarters will have their own planning standard
operating instructions for describing the conduct of the process within their
organisations. The output of the OPP is a set of orders that communicates the
plan to subordinate commanders. The OPP is illustrated in UK Figure G.1.

           problem?                        Understand the situation
                                            and frame the problem

 AJP-5



                 Preparation for the operations planning process – plan to plan

                 UK G.2. ‘Plan the planning’ is a vital function to ensure the process is
                 successful. Key considerations include the following.

                           a. Context. Is the planning event to be conducted to deal with an
                           emerging crisis or to develop contingency plans? A crisis is likely to
                           have a Chief of the Defence Staff/Chief of Joint Operations directive
                           (verbal, draft or signed) to instigate planning. A contingency plan
                           may have no strategic direction. This will have an impact on the
                           conduct of the initiation and mission analysis described below.

                           b. When. J5 analyse the time available for planning and identify
                           key milestones at the start, such as the mission analysis briefing
                           and COA decision brief, which is best illustrated on a planning
                           timeline. Headquarters planning and delivering orders should take
                           no more than one third of the time available, leaving two thirds for
                           subordinate headquarters’ planning.

                           c. Who. Establish the size and composition of the joint operations
                           planning group (JOPG). Ensure you have representation from all
                           key stakeholders (subordinate commanders, other government
                           departments, allies and partners, and so on).

                           d. How. Record keeping and information management is crucial,
                           especially to capture requests for information, assumptions and
                           risks, for example, from the start. The process typically produces
                           a mass of data and seeking to try and generate everything
                           electronically is often not the best answer. Traditional methods
                           (for example, using whiteboards, flip charts and paper) can aid
                           planning group dynamics and ensure they are most productive,
                           especially when under significant time pressure. The rapid advance
                           of technology will allow organisations to adapt and accelerate the
                           planning process.

                           e. What. This should account for what products will be required.
                           Planning and operational timelines should be developed (being
                           cognisant of the existing battle rhythm), showing who owes what
                           to whom and when (inputs and outputs of each stage). Be ruthless
UK                         with allocating the time available and enforce time management.
G

                                                G-2
                             Edition B Version 1 + UK national element
                                                                                    UK Annex G to
                                                                                           AJP-5



           Other products include an initial task and requests for information
           tracker, record of assumptions and a risk matrix.

Receipt of orders brief

UK G.3. The receipt of orders brief is an internal trigger event within an
operational headquarters to start a planning process. This step is not explicitly
defined in operational-level doctrine but is critical to conduct successful
planning and will be context dependent. It is usually delivered to staff by the
command group and may contain the following.

          1. Alert staff, key advisers and liaison officers (cross-government,
          allies and partners) of the requirement to plan and inform them of the
          mission/likely mission.

          2.   Ensure the headquarters understands:
               o what the headquarters is being asked to do;
               o the context in which it is being asked to do it; and
               o what the output of the planning process will be.

          3. Assign planning teams and provide any preliminary direction or
          higher-level context.

          4.   Align staff with the strategic narrative.

          5.   Review doctrine relevant to the forthcoming operation.

          6. Ensure commonality of planning language amongst the
          headquarters.

          7. Review lessons from previous similar operations, or if they are
          not immediately available draw them down from wider Defence
          lessons organisations.

          8. Outline initial operational and planning timelines, which will be
          further refined during planning.

          9. Initiate the planning guidance and information management
          processes.

          10. Branch leads will then be granted the opportunity to comment
                                                                                               UK
          on initial key deductions.
                                                                                               G

                                              G-3
                          Edition B Version 1 + UK national elements
 UK Annex G to
 AJP-5



                 Section 1 – Initiation
                 UK G.4. The two key outcomes of initiation are to understand:

                              • why are we where we are (why are we looking at this problem);
                                and

                              • who is doing what and why in the operating environment.

                 UK Figure G.2 summaries the key inputs and outputs of the initiation stage, as
                 well as the main planning activity that takes place within the headquarters.

UK G.5. Operational and planning timelines will be refined from the receipt of
orders brief. To frame the problem correctly, you will need to understand the
current, or predicted, situation (where we are) by initiating the joint intelligence
preparation of the operating environment, and analysing the strategic context,
the operating environment and the actors involved to understand who is
doing what and why. Additionally, to frame correctly, you need to understand
what the desired situation (where we want to be) looks like (analysis of higher
commander’s intent, military strategic objectives, strategic/military strategic
centres of gravity (CoGs) and so on). This will also ensure the military plan
nests within the comprehensive approach, across all the levers of power, and
across all operational domains. There are many different tools available to
support this analysis and some of the common ones are listed below.

           •   PMESII-PT: political, military, economic, social, infrastructure,
               information, physical environment, time.

           •   PESTLE (I): political, economic, social, technological, legal,
               environmental, (information).

           •   Constituents of a state: rule of law, education, commercial,
               humanitarian, health, information, military, economic, diplomacy,
               administration, governance.

           •   STEEPLEM: social, technological, economic, environmental,
               political, legal, ethical, military.

           •   ASCOPE: area, structure, capabilities, organisation, people,
               events. This is a useful way of analysing the output from the tools
               above.

A number of other recognised tools and techniques include the following.

           •   Human terrain/operational domain analysis.

           •   Multi-perspective diagrams.

           •   Context diagrams.

           •   Concept maps and network analysis.
                                                                                                  UK
           •   Diplomatic, information, military, economic, financial and legal                   G
               (DIME-FL) analysis.
                                              G-5
                          Edition B Version 1 + UK national elements
 UK Annex G to
 AJP-5



                 Section 2 – Mission analysis

                 strategic context, effects and conditions to produce an initial operations
                 design. UK Figure G.3 summaries the key inputs and outputs of the mission
                 analysis stage as well as the main planning activity that takes place within the
                 headquarters.

Mission analysis – four questions

UK G.7. Question 1 – what is the strategic intent? Analyse the superior
authorities’ directives to understand their end state and objectives. (For
example, in the UK this would be Chief of the Defence Staff and Chief of Joint
Operations directives, in NATO, this would be a North Atlantic Council Initiating
Directive and Supreme Allied Commander Europe’s Strategic Planning
Directive). These will set the boundaries of the problem.

UK G.8. Question 2 – what is my role? What are the future desired
operational end state and objectives? What conditions, effects and activities
are required to contribute to the realisation of my superior commander’s
intent? You will need to analyse:

          •   the DIME-FL to understand my part in the strategic context;

          •   operational end state;

          •   operational objectives;

          •   my mission;

          •   my specified and implied tasks, leading to decisive conditions,
              operational effects and operational actions; and

          •   potential operational-level CoGs; adversaries', other actors' and
              own.

These taken together should deliver the outcomes determined in Question 1.

UK G.9. Question 3 – do I have the freedoms, resources and authority
required to attain my operational end state? Consider this in terms of time
and space and narrative. How is the problem bounded? You will need to
analyse:

     •   freedoms;
     •   limitations;
     •   constraints (something you must do);
     •   restraints (something you must not do); and
     •   resources.
                                                                                               UK
                                                                                               G

                                             G-7
                         Edition B Version 1 + UK national elements
 UK Annex G to
 AJP-5



                 UK G.10. Question 4 – Has the situation changed or how might it change? If
                 the situation has changed, has this affected the overall outcomes sought, or how
                 could it (risk)? Can I make any provision now for how the situation might change
                 in the future? This should provide the basis for contingency plans (CONPLANs).

                 UK G.11. The outputs from the four questions create the operational-level
                 planning building blocks. This is a circular process breaking down the problem
                 through the planning on the left hand side, then building back up through the
                 execution/outcomes on the right hand side, as illustrated at UK Figure G.4.

                       Operational     outcome that is essential to a commander's plan and towards

Centre of gravity analysis

UK G.12. CoG analysis helps to determine the focus of the planning and
the tasks that need to be carried out to attain your end state. CoG analysis is
illustrated in UK Figure G.5.


                 Actor’s assessed aim and desired outcome: (ends)
  What is the actor’s main goal and what conditions do they seek to achieve by their actions?


              Centre of gravity:                             Critical (cap)abilities: (ways)

       ...is the principal source of strength             ...what critical capabilities, motivations
         or power for achieving one’s aim.                   and opportunities must the actor
                                                              possess to achieve their aim?

                                                       The key word is the verb ‘the ability to...’.


           Critical vulnerabilities:                      Critical requirements: (means)

                       UK Figure G.5 – Centre of gravity analysis

UK G.13. The CoG analysis produces two key outputs. These are: critical
vulnerabilities and the CoG itself.

             a. Critical vulnerabilities. Critical vulnerabilities to be attacked,
             defended or manipulated.

             b. The centre of gravity. What primary entity, selected from the
             critical requirements, provides an organisation with its critical ability
             to achieve its aim. What is it that needs to be weakened in an
             adversary, defended in ourselves or allies/partners or manipulated
             in other actors?
                                                                                                                  UK
                                                                                                                  G

                                                    G-9
                                Edition B Version 1 + UK national elements
 UK Annex G to
 AJP-5



                 UK G.14. Critical vulnerabilities. The process for deducing the critical
                 vulnerabilities is as follows.

                            a. Identify the aim/intent of the actor being analysed (the ‘ends’).
                            This should be drawn from the analysis carried out in the initiation
                            phase. If time allows, a CoG analysis should be done to address
                            the aim in the ‘most likely’ or ‘most dangerous’ COA of a particular
                            actor. Thus, there could be more than one CoG analysis for each
                            actor. This aim should be bounded in time and space if possible.

                            b. Identify the critical capabilities that an actor must possess to
                            be able to achieve that aim (the ‘ways’). Each of these should be
                            started with the words: 'the ability to' ….

                            c. Identify the critical requirements of each of those capabilities
                            (the ‘means’). Each of these should be started with the words:
                            'needs' ….

                            d. Identify the critical vulnerabilities within the critical requirements
                            that can be exploited across all operational domains. These should
                            be phrased in terms of the effect required.

                            e. The critical vulnerabilities may be further analysed (perhaps by
                            three-column format) to determine the best ways to exploit them.
                            This is an excellent opportunity to think across the spectrum of
                            military and civilian capabilities.

                 UK G.15. Centre of gravity. A deduced CoG can be derived by selecting
                 the primary entity from the list of critical requirements and asking which of the
                 requirements has the inherent capability to perform the ‘ways’? If there are
                 multiple candidates, they will need to be prioritised to select the primary one.
                 The ‘real’ CoG is supported; it is the ‘doer’. Other candidates are supporting.
                 The CoG is inherently capable of achieving the purpose or objective and
                 executes the primary action(s) that achieves it. It uses or consumes supporting
                 resources to achieve the objective. If something is ‘used’ or consumed to
                 execute the primary action, it is a requirement. If it contributes to, but does
                 not actually perform, the action, it serves a supporting function and is a
                 requirement. Ultimately does the deduced CoG give the actor the ability to fulfil
                 the ‘ways’ (critical capabilities)? In this construct, there are no ‘moral’ CoGs,
UK               only physical ones. Intangibles, such as moral strength, public opinion, or a
G                righteous cause, are not CoGs because they have no inherent capability for


                                                G-10
                             Edition B Version 1 + UK national element
                                                                                  UK Annex G to
                                                                                         AJP-5



action. However, they are not without value and they can be requirements.
A tangible physical agent must perform the action.

Factor analysis

UK G.16. All key factors that have a bearing on the mission and activities
in the joint operations area should be analysed most often using the
three-column tabular format, as illustrated in UK Figure G.6, and the key
outputs presented at the mission analysis briefing. As a minimum, the following
topics and issues need to be covered in the factor analysis.

          •   Outputs from theatre recce and host nation coordination.

          •   Inputs from staff branch functional estimates, for example:

              o outputs from the initiation phase;

              o time;

              o space;

              o forces (own, adversary and in the wider joint operations area);

              o information;

              o J4 logistic estimate;

              o engineer/infrastructure estimate;

              o command and control estimate;

              o J6 estimate;

              o   medical estimate;

              o G9 policy and legal issues; and

              o Defence strategic communication/public affairs/media.

UK G.18. The risk register is used to track the risks and the implementation
of the mitigation throughout planning and execution. The risk matrix provides
a visual representation of the risks and their trend; it is useful to display the key
risks on one matrix to provide a better understanding of the cumulative risk to
the operation, as shown in UK Figure G.7

Operations design

UK G.19. Operations design provides the critical link between operational
problems to be solved and the required operational outcomes. It takes the
results of framing the problem (from initiation and mission analysis) and
develops and refines the commander’s vision. The commander’s vision is the
expression of operational art and the commander’s intent is their personal
vision for the conduct of the operation. The operations design translates this
vision into a conceptual overview of the entire operations by establishing
decisive conditions along different lines of operation, leading to the achieving
                                                                                                                                                     UK
the operational objectives and attaining the desired end state (the expression                                                                       G
of operational science).
                                                         G-13
                                      Edition B Version 1 + UK national elements
 UK Annex G to
 AJP-5



                 Writing an intent UK

                 UK G.20. The commander’s intent describes the overall outcome of what you
                 want to achieve and why. It is constructed in the following way.

                             •   Describe the objectives and the effects using time or space to
                                 group them, with concurrent or sequential activity (method) –
                                 do not put troops to task yet.

                             •   A statement of the key risks that may impact the operation.

                             •   Summarise what success looks like and indicate what next (the
                                 end state), identify exit strategy/exit criteria.

                             •   An intent should explain what the commander wants to achieve
                                 and why – the change required in a given situation to be
                                 delivered by the operation; it binds the forces together and sets
                                 the tone for what is to follow.

                 Initial operations design

                 UK G.21. The initial operations design is a schematic developed by the
                 joint operations planning group, drawing together the results of the mission
                 analysis. Developing the initial operations design is an iterative process and it
                 will be further developed and refined during COA development. Its purpose
                 is to communicate the commander’s vision of the operation, provide a
                 common basis for the development of COAs, be used as a tool to integrate,
                 synchronise, coordinate, prioritise and allocate capabilities for the operation
                 over time, assess the progress of the operation, refine plans to deal with
                 foreseen and unforeseen events and develop initial ideas for the transition and
                 termination of the operation. Developing the initial operations design includes
                 the following steps.

                             •   Extract potential decisive conditions from the analysis to form a
                                 list of proposed decisive conditions.

                             •   Determine the sequence in which specific decisive conditions
                                 must be established.

UK                           •   Group decisive conditions into types of activity which will form
G                                potential lines of operations to focus the effort required to
                                 achieve one or more operational objectives.
                                                G-14
                             Edition B Version 1 + UK national element
                                                                                                                                                                                 UK Annex G to
                                                                                                                                                                                        AJP-5



              •   Add operational effects that achieve the decisive conditions;
                  draft effect measurement mechanisms.

              •   Add any identified operational actions that create the
                  operational effects; draft measures of performance (operational
                  actions will be developed much further in COA development).

              •   Sequence and phase the decisive conditions, effects and
                  actions. This helps maintain the operational continuity and
                  tempo, and avoids unnecessary operational pauses.

              •   Determine branches and sequels – the joint operations planning
                  group may identity decision points that lead to the requirement
                  for branches, sequels and potential CONPLANs.

An example operations design schematic is shown in UK Figure G.8.

                 UK G.22. Mission analysis briefing. The mission analysis briefing is a fulcrum
                 moment that pulls together all the analysis to date that allows the commander
                 to develop COAs. Its purpose is to validate the mission analysis, including
                 confirming the mission and its operational objectives, and the initial operations
                 design. It is also a forum where the entire staff and subordinate command
                 liaison officers can be present to ensure a unified vision in preparation for
                 developing operational COAs. The commander can confirm an initial intent
                 and guidance for developing COAs and for the orientation of subordinate
                 headquarters. The commander must eventually take ownership of the following.

                            •   The assigned mission, including operational objectives, and
                                any recommendations for change from those provided by the
                                superior commander in their directive.

                            •   The initial operations design, including lines of operations, and
                                the sequence of required decisive conditions in different phases
                                of the operation.

                            •   Decisive conditions and operational effects.

                            •   Operational actions.

                            •   Assumptions and risks

                            •   Confirming the commander’s intent.

                            •   Provisional missions to subordinate commanders.

                            •   The different COAs, in broad terms, to be developed as a basis
                                for further planning.

Section 3 – Course of action
development
Purpose of course of action development

UK G.23. The purpose of COA development is to develop a set of draft
COAs in accordance with the commander’s intent. This process is illustrated
in UK Figure G.9. Using outputs from analysis carried out thus far, the outline
COAs are developed by the staff for the commander’s review prior to them
being developed, refined and analysed. This gives the commander a chance to
ensure COAs meet expectations at an early stage, rule out or alter COAs and
give further direction and guidance. The COAs should answer the following
questions about the operations design.

           •   Who will conduct each operational action to create operational
               effects to achieve decisive conditions?

           •   What operational actions are being considered?

           •   When does each action begin and by when must it be
               complete?

           •   Where will the actions be performed?

           •   Why is each action being performed (link to operational effects
               and decisive conditions)?

           •   How will the actions be conducted?

                 UK G.24. Developing courses of action. The headquarters reconfigures into
                 cross-function COA development teams who will conduct the following.

                                 •   Begin by discussing ways to accomplish the tasks associated
                                     with the mission – the staff should think, in an integrated
                                     context, two levels down and plan one level down.

                                 •   The J2 team will develop the key actors' most likely COA and
                                     most dangerous COA for the staff to plan against.

                                 •   COAs must draw forward from the mission analysis all
                                     operational effects and operational actions to achieve decisive
                                     conditions.

UK                               •   Identify possible phases of the COA by roughly sequencing the
G                                    decisive conditions, operational effects and operational actions.


                                                     G-18
                                  Edition B Version 1 + UK national element
                                                                                   UK Annex G to
                                                                                          AJP-5



               Work out who will conduct each action – considering military
               forces and cross-government capabilities (phase lines will often
               be aligned with the transition of supported versus supporting
               components).

           •   Where will the actions be performed and effects be created?
               At what vulnerabilities are the actions being directed?

Deception

UK G.25. Deception should be included and resourced from early in the
planning process. The following questions should be considered.

           •   What does the adversary expect me to do?
           •   How are their actions relevant to my COAs?
           •   How can I use deception to my advantage?

UK G.26.   Doctrinally the acronym STOP is used. This steps are shown below.

           •   Story: ‘What do you want the deception target to believe?’

           •   Target: ‘Whose sensors (see), cognitions (think) and behaviour
               (do) are you trying to deceive?’

           •   Objective: ‘What behaviour do you want the deception target to
               do (action) or not do (inaction)?’

           •   Plan: ‘How you plan and execute the deception, as part of the
               overall plan and its objectives?’

UK G.27. In addition to STOP, the ‘see–think–do’ model can assist deception
planning. The steps are shown below.

           •   See – the deceptive information, intelligence and activities the
               deception target needs to see (sensing).

           •   Think – how the deception target thinks about what they see
               (sense), via their cognitive processes (memories, assumptions,
               expectations, beliefs, perceptions and attitudes).
                                                                                              UK
           • Do – the behaviours the deception target is required to perform,                 G
             expressed as either action (do something) or inaction (do nothing).
                                            G-19
                         Edition B Version 1 + UK national elements
 UK Annex G to
 AJP-5



                 The model is used forwards to sell the deception to the deception target
                 (see–think–do), but it is planned backwards (do–think–see), which is to say,
                 for the deception target to undertake the desired behaviour they need to think
                 about the situation; and to make the deception target think about the situation
                 they need to see (sense) information, intelligence and activities. UK Figure G.10
                 summarises this process.


                                                   Planned this way
                                           Start with the desired action / inaction



                         See                           Think                             Do
                         What do I need                What do I need the         What do I need the
                         the deception target          deception target           deception target to
                         to see (sensing) in           to think in order          do that will help friendly
                         order to shape their          to act in the way          forces accomplish
                         perception of reality?        we need?                   the mission?


                                                  Executed this way
                        Pre-planned deception event execution driving desired action / inaction




                                     UK Figure G.10 – The see–think–do model

                 UK G.28. Viability test. Each COA will be tested for its viability using the
                 feasibility, acceptability, completeness, compliance, exclusivity and suitability
                 (FACCES) test.

                             •    Feasibility – is the COA possible within the operating
                                  environment and given time, space and resources?

                             • Acceptability – do the COA benefits outweigh the costs?
                               (consider casualties, collateral effects, media and public
                               reaction).

                             •    Completeness – is the COA complete? (when, who, what,
                                  why and how?)

                             •    Compliance – is the COA compliant with NATO doctrine, policy
UK                                and legal guidance?
G

                                                 G-20
                              Edition B Version 1 + UK national element
                                                                                                        UK Annex G to
                                                                                                               AJP-5



              •    Exclusivity – is the COA sufficiently different from other
                   COAs to clearly differentiate competitive advantages and
                   disadvantages?

              •    Suitability – will the COA accomplish the mission and does it
                   comply with the commander’s planning guidance?

Example course of action briefing format

UK G.29. Visual storyboards are a very effective way of displaying COA
concepts. An example format is illustrated in UK Figure G.11; whilst the exact
format may vary between headquarters, each COA should use the same
format for ease of comparison.

              •    The COAs will not be complete by the end of this stage.
                   Only enough detail is required to satisfy the validity test and
                   commander’s review. Development and refinement will continue
                   through the next stages.

              •    The commander may choose to narrow or expand the range of
                   COAs and determine selection criteria.

                 Section 4 – Course of action analysis
                 UK G.30. The aim of this stage is to refine and analyse the COAs in

                  UK Figure G.12 – Inputs, outputs and staff activity during course of action
                                                  analysis

                 UK G.31. This stage is about synchonising effects and actions and
                 turning them into missions and tasks for subordinate commanders. A range
                 of planning tools will be used to illustrate the timelines, sequencing and
                 troops to task and this will be captured in the synchronisation matrix. The
                 synchronisation process is an important part of coordinating the actions
                 across all operational domains to create maximum effect in a given time or
                 space. Once the COA has been refined and analysed it will be war gamed to
                 test the plan, or elements of the plan, against the actor’s most likely or most
UK               dangerous COA. The key outputs of this stage are listed below.
G

                                                     G-22
                                  Edition B Version 1 + UK national element
                                                                                 UK Annex G to
                                                                                        AJP-5



          •   Refined effects schematic and tables.

          •   Decisive conditions and operational effects tables (including
              measurement of effect).

          •   Integration tables.

          •   Synchronisation matrices.

          •   Examples and further explanation of these products can be
              found at UK Annex E.

Additional analysis tools

UK G.32. The COAs will describe complex plans. These can be further
refined using the following analysis tools.

           a. Wargaming. Wargaming is a flexible instrument designed to
           develop, compare and improve COAs. It can be used at any time
           during COA development as a way of testing elements of the plan
           against the key actors’ most likely COA and most dangerous COA.

           b. Red teaming. Red teaming must be embedded in the
           planning process from the start. The team should scrutinise
           and challenge all aspects of the plan, test assumptions and
           consider the perspective of the adversary to highlight threats and
           consequences of each COA.

           c. Premortem. This comes before a plan is implemented so that
           the plan can be improved before execution, rather than autopsied
           after failure. The premortem assumes the plan was executed
           and failed, then asks the question: ‘what went wrong?’ The team
           is then tasked to think of all the plausible reasons for the plan’s
           failure.

                                                                                            G

                       Edition B Version 1 + UK national elements

                 Section 5 – Course of action validation
                 and comparison
                 UK G.33. Purpose of course of action validation and comparison. The aim
                 of this stage is to use validation and comparison of the COAs to determine,
                 and develop a justification for, a single COA to be recommended for selection.
                 This stage is illustrated in UK Figure G.13. COAs should be considered
                 independently of each other and evaluated against the set of criteria
                 established by the staff and/or the commander. The comparison process is
                 always subjective. The planners should recommend the COA with:

                 highest probability of success + acceptable level of risk

UK G.34. Comparison of course of action methods. There are a number of
different methods and criteria for making the comparison. The COA evaluation
criteria should be drafted by the planners, presented to the commander,
refined and rated according to direction and then used in the COA comparison
process. Examples include:

           •   descriptive comparison – inherent advantages/disadvantages;

           •   performance against commander’s selection criteria (weighting
               applied afterwards);

           •   performance against key actors’ most dangerous COA and
               most likely COA; and

           •   COA risk assessment – risk versus benefit balance.

When comparing COAs, it is worth noting the following points.

           •   This is not an exact science; the numerically superior COA may
               not always be the best recommendation.

           •   Weighting of commander’s criteria should not be known by
               those making the assessment – it is always added after scoring
               to stop inadvertent bias.

           •   The most important aspect is to be able to explain to the
               commander why one COA, or an element of a COA, is
               preferred over another.

           •   A high scoring element of one COA may be used to strengthen
               another COA.

                                                                                           G

                 Section 6 – Commander’s course of
                 action decision
                 Purpose

                 UK G.35. The purpose of this stage is to obtain a commander’s decision
                 on a chosen COA to move forward to develop the concept of operations
                 (CONOPS). The process is illustrated in UK Figure G.14. The commander may
                 select a COA with or without modification, or may direct that additional COAs
                 be investigated.
